### Text

🛡️ Vulnerability in ERC4626 with incorrect reward accounting

In the Yield Basis protocol, the share calculation in LiquidityGauge happens before processing pending AMM rewards. This leads to using outdated totalAssets values.

Because of this, users can withdraw more assets than they should, and repeated restaking increases their share, diluting honest participants.

The problem lies in the incorrect order between share calculation and reward accounting, which poses a risk of economic damage.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://x.com/sherlockdefi/status/2007072383690231990?s=20

### Text

🛡️ Vulnerability in Super DCA: LP Rewards Theft

In the Super DCA protocol, rewards are distributed only based on the current price position, ignoring who actually held liquidity during the accumulation period.

An attacker with a narrow liquidity range manipulates the price to claim all the rewards at the time of distribution, leaving honest LPs unrewarded.

The problem lies in the lack of accounting for the time a position spent in the range and tick manipulations before the distribution.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://x.com/sherlockdefi/status/2004537760942903672

🛡️ Ledger Data Leak: New Security Risks

A recent leak of Ledger customers' personal data exposed names, addresses, phone numbers, and emails, but private keys and recovery phrases were not compromised.

Attackers have started to directly use this data for phishing, spoofed calls, and QR-code traps, increasing the risk of asset loss.

A particular danger is the possibility of physical attacks on users whose addresses are known.

Protection now requires masking your email, avoiding SMS-based authentication, and strict verification of every transaction on the device.

- **Media:** photo
- **Views:** 394

### Text

⛽ Gas estimation errors in TON lead to critical failures

TON uses a complex fee model and asynchronous messages, where correct gas estimation is crucial to maintaining state consistency.

If a contract lacks sufficient funds to pay for bounce messages, they are not sent, and the state becomes inconsistent, leading to loss of funds.

With TVM 12, the cost of bounce messages has increased, meaning that fee calculations need to be even more conservative.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://www.chainsecurity.com/blog/ton-gas-estimation

- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security
- **Media:** photo
- **Views:** 369

### Text

🔐 Soundness Errors in ZK Schemes and Their Risks

ZK schemes require precise constraints on computations; otherwise, an attacker can prove false statements.

A common issue is insufficient binding of computed values to public outputs, which gives the attacker freedom to manipulate.

It is also important to explicitly define the semantics of integer operations and ranges, otherwise proofs can be forged.

Real-world cases like zkSync Era and Kakarot demonstrate the consequences of such errors — the ability to modify critical data without breaking the proof.

Overall, any missing check is a potential vulnerability that allows forging proofs.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://muellerberndt.medium.com/finding-soundness-bugs-in-zk-circuits-ea23387a0e1e

### Text

🏦 Critical Vulnerability in RAI’s Liquidation Mechanism

A bug has been discovered in RAI that allows an attacker to block position liquidations via a returndata bomb attack — sending excessively large data in a revert, causing an out of gas error.

As a result, positions remain non-liquidatable, leading to the accumulation of bad debt and risking the insolvency of the protocol.

Governance acknowledged the bug but classified it as "out of scope" because of the involvement of governance validation — which allows them to avoid paying a bug bounty.

### Text

🛡️ New protection against overflow errors in Go

By default, Go ignores overflow in integer arithmetic operations, which hides serious vulnerabilities.

The go-panikint project is a modified Go compiler that turns such overflows into explicit panics at runtime.

This helps to find real bugs, like in Cosmos SDK RPC pagination, revealing previously invisible issues during fuzzing.

🔗 Details

- **Views:** 399

### Text

🌉 Web2.5 Vulnerability in Cross-Chain Swaps

A hole appeared in the chain between the backend and on-chain: when a transaction with an incorrect signature fails, the backend logic reveals the secret in the public calldata.

This allows an attacker to simultaneously obtain funds on both chains — a double spend.

The fix checks signatures, but due to mismatches in Go and Solidity libraries, attackers can bypass validation by repeatedly triggering the secret leak.

Conclusion: differences in off-chain and on-chain validations create critical risks in Web2.5 protocols.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://blog.decurity.io/a-web2-5-vulnerability-story-3d0cc5a4d58f

- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security
- **Media:** photo
- **Views:** 446

### Text

🛡️ Proper use of require in DeFi for protocol security

Instead of checking conditions only inside functions, it’s worth applying the FREI-PI approach, which combines function checks and key protocol invariants.

This helps protect DeFi protocols from errors that violate the main security rules, for example, preventing a state with an unsafe lending position.

The classic Checks-Effects-Interactions pattern is useful but too local and does not guarantee the preservation of the core protocol invariant.

FREI-PI allows all operations to be done within a single transaction while maintaining the security of the entire protocol.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://www.nascent.xyz/idea/youre-writing-require-statements-wrong

🛡️ Move Smart Contract Audit Checklist

Auditing Move requires understanding the specific features of each network — Sui, Aptos, and IOTA differ in architecture and vulnerabilities.

Key measures include thorough verification of access rights, control over object ownership, and protection against logical errors (overflows, division issues, and atomicity).

Special attention should be paid to handling external calls, resource management, and restricting hardcoded addresses to prevent attacks.

Unique risks: parallel transactions in Sui, borrow operations in Aptos, and batching in IOTA require specialized checks.

### Text

🧮 Rounding Issues in DeFi

Rounding errors in DeFi lead to vulnerabilities and attacks, as seen in Balancer and Bunny.

A simple rule — rounding in favor of the protocol — is insufficient due to complex formulas and the reuse of values.

Incorrect rounding is always a bug that can lead to an exploit.

It is important to design formulas so that rounding errors compensate each other and to control the direction of rounding at every step.

🔗 Details

- https://seceureka.com/blog/rounding-in-defi

### Text

🚧 x402: A New Standard for Secure Payment HTTP Requests

x402 activates HTTP 402 for instant payments in Web3, utilizing EIP-712 and ERC-3009 to protect against replay attacks.

It is important to consider vulnerabilities: MITM attacks on unencrypted requests can alter payment parameters, and poor nonce management creates risks of collisions and front-running.

Additionally, autonomous agent keys require strict protection using HD wallets and spending limits.

Security depends on a combination of cryptography, parameter validation, and continuous monitoring.

🔗 Details

- https://blog.valkyrisec.com/x402-integration-security/

### Text

⚡️ The New Role of L2 in Ethereum

L2s did not become “branded shards” due to complexities and the growing scalability of L1 with low fees.

Ethereum now scales directly on L1 by increasing gas limits and reducing dependence on L2.

L2 should be seen as a spectrum of solutions with varying levels of connection to Ethereum — from full security to specialized features.

The main point is to look for unique capabilities in L2, not just scaling: privacy, speed, new models, or scaling outside of finance.

### Text

🛡️ Web3 Security Highlights: Key Takeaways from 2025

In 2025, EIP-7702 changed the rules for EOAs, opening new attack vectors through code delegation, which led to major exploits and millions of dollars stolen.

Ethereum updates Pectra and Fusaka improved scalability and functionality but introduced risks of centralization and possibilities for new vulnerabilities.

Significant attacks affected Bybit ($1.4 billion), Balancer ($121 million), as well as complex bugs in protocols involving rounding errors and logic flaws that caused multimillion-dollar losses.

There were also large-scale supply chain compromises involving malicious code, as well as bugs in Rust libraries and on the Beacon Chain impacting validators.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://www.openzeppelin.com/news/web3-security-auditors-2025-rewind

### Text

🛡️ Gyroscope Hack: $807K Stolen

On January 30, 2026, a vulnerability was discovered in the Gyroscope bridge involving arbitrary input, which allowed a hacker to gain unlimited approval of GYD tokens on Ethereum.

The attacker withdrew 6 million GYD (~$807K) using transferFrom(), despite limited liquidity. Defenders offered a 33% white hat bug bounty — a record-high reward.

The main flaw was the lack of recipient address verification, which enabled the attack via an approve() call on the token contract.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://www.certik.com/resources/blog/gyroscope-incident-analysis

🛡️ Checking Anchor Constraints in Solana

Anchor is a key framework for Solana development that verifies accounts through constraints. Mistakes in these can lead to vulnerabilities like broken access control or DoS.

The tool anchor-constraints-analyzer speeds up audits by visualizing relationships between accounts and identifying undefined or insufficiently verified addresses.

This helps quickly uncover potential issues in program logic. It’s especially useful for complex projects where manual analysis of constraints takes a lot of time.

### Text

🧠 AI in Web3 Security: How to Reduce Randomness

AI vulnerability research is unstable: the same model can find a bug on one run and miss it on another when analyzing code repeatedly.

The tool zkao addresses this problem by repeating the analyses over and over, similar to fuzzing, increasing the chances of detecting errors.

This is especially important for audits in complex systems like Circom, where the team has extensive experience. Constant repeated analysis reduces the role of luck and improves the quality of security.

🔗 Details

- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security
- **Media:** photo
- **Views:** 390

### Text

⚙️ Fusaka Update: Important Ethereum Security Changes

Fusaka introduces a gas limit per transaction — a maximum of about 16.78 million gas, which may cause failures for "heavy" smart contracts. 

The limits and costs of the MODEXP operations have been changed, affecting gas usage and potentially causing errors when gas is fixed.

A new opcode CLZ has been added for optimization, but it may cause issues on L2 and has a special case when the input is 0.

A precompile P256VERIFY has been added for verifying signatures on secp256r1, important for cross-platform security.

Paying attention to these updates will help avoid failures and maintain smart contract security.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://blog.sigmaprime.io/fusaka-contract-security.html

### Text

🛡️ Main Security Vulnerabilities of the L2 Base for Web3

We have identified 5 serious blind spots in the Base protocols: sequencer censorship without fallback, lack of fraud proofs, outdated cross-chain callbacks, vulnerable paymasters, and double-spending risks on the bridge.

These issues stem from Base’s characteristics — a single sequencer, 7-day finalization delay, and asynchronous cross-chain messages — which don’t exist on Ethereum L1.

Each problem has proven solutions: fallback to L1, fraud-proof safeguards, strict nonces and timelocks, operation limits, and atomic bridge calls.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://x.com/QuillAudits_AI/status/2019794305238585391

### Text

🛠️ Main Vulnerabilities of Prediction Markets

In half of the reviewed projects, reentrancy bugs were found due to the transfer of ERC1155 tokens occurring in the middle of a function call.

Imprecise matching of the YES + NO price to 1 USDC leads to losses because of order book spreads and incorrect payout distribution when there is a negative yield.

Charging fees through YES or NO tokens creates a risk of losses if the market moves sharply; fees should be converted immediately to USDC.

Position volatility and the lack of reliable oracles complicate lending and risk assessment.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://paragraph.com/@0x3b/pm-bugs

### Text

⚖️ Rounding error of liquidity in Uniswap v3/v4

In Uniswap, when adding liquidity, modifyLiquidity rounds token amounts up, while commonly used functions always round down.

Because of this, the logic can underestimate the spent tokens by up to 1 wei per position, which affects balance calculations and refunds.

It is recommended to consider the actual BalanceDelta returned by modifyLiquidity, rather than the results of helper functions.

🔗 Details

- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security
- **Media:** photo
- **Views:** 415

### Text

🛡 Critical bug in Solidity 0.8.28–0.8.33 involving clearing of transient and persistent storage

In Solidity versions 0.8.28 through 0.8.33, when using the IR pipeline, due to a collision of helper function names, opcodes for transient and persistent storage get mixed up.

This leads to incorrect clearing: transient delete may overwrite important persistent storage data, while persistent delete does not clear the state properly.

The issue affects only contracts compiled with the --via-ir flag and has been fixed in version 0.8.34.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://www.soliditylang.org/blog/2026/02/18/transient-storage-clearing-helper-collision-bug/

🛡️ 9 Most Common Vault Bugs from Real Audits

Frequent errors are related to incorrect accounting of shares and assets, which allows attackers to steal funds or sabotage yields.

Issues arise during the first deposit, mistakes with rewards, and front-running bad debt, as well as improper handling of limits and asset locks.

Incorrect calculations and rounding can lead to vault insolvency due to “dust” (minimal residuals), and the order of withdrawals reduces users’ earnings.

Correct implementation requires careful tracking of all tokens and consideration of the business logic behind distribution.

### Text

🚨 Critical vulnerability in a DeFi inheritance protocol

In the protocol’s code that simulates a digital will, one inconspicuous function without access restrictions allowed an attacker to infinitely "reset" the owner’s activity timer.

Because of this, the protocol always considers the user active, and the heirs cannot access the funds — the inheritance is locked forever.

The fix was simple — restrict the function call to the owner only. Any external function is a potential attack vector, even if it "just updates a name."

🔗 Details

### Text

🔐 Google API Keys Became Vulnerable Due to Gemini

Google long considered API keys not as secrets, but merely as identifiers, safe for public exposure.

With the launch of Gemini, such keys automatically gained access to sensitive data and APIs, despite being publicly posted.

This opened the door to an attack allowing theft of data and withdrawal of funds simply by copying a key from open source code.

More than 2,800 such keys have been found online, including Google keys, confirming the severity of the issue.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://trufflesecurity.com/blog/google-api-keys-werent-secrets-but-then-gemini-changed-the-rules

### Text

🔥 $11M stolen from YieldBlox pool via a single market with invisible liquidity

The attack on Blend V2 occurred due to distorted prices on a low-volume collateral USTRY — just one trade with a price 100 times above the market triggered the oracle’s trust.

The smart contract code had no vulnerabilities, but the zero-liquidity market, lack of anomaly checks, and blind trust in the oracle led to a loss of $10.97M.

The attack was carefully planned, funds were quickly drained through bridges and partially frozen by Stellar validators.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://rekt.news/yieldblox-rekt

- **Media:** photo
- **Views:** 368

### Text

💥 Attack on Curve Finance: Price Manipulation

The hack of Curve Finance is related to a vulnerability in the liquidation mechanism of Curve Lend and the price oracles.

The attacker first performed a soft liquidation, forcing users to "sell" their collateral, then pushed the price via the sDOLA pool, and finally completed a hard liquidation with profit.

The problem is that the soft liquidation is based on oracles that sharply change, causing unexpected losses even when the price is rising.

Inverse Finance was unaffected, while users of Curve Lend were impacted.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://x.com/hklst4r/status/2028420263642075416

- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security
- **Media:** photo
- **Views:** 370

### Text

🛡️ Vulnerabilities in 6 zkVMs: how proof verification is broken

Critical bugs were discovered in six zkVMs (Jolt, Nexus, Cairo-M, Ceno, Expander, Binius64), where the values being verified are not included in the Fiat-Shamir transcript in time. 

This allows an attacker to forge public claims and bypass cryptographic protection.

The issue is related to the violation of the data processing order in the transcript — the program can choose forged values after the random challenges appear, breaking the soundness of the proofs.

In all cases, the fix is simple — adding 1–2 lines of code to correctly include the data in the transcript, but detecting the problem is difficult without deep analysis.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://osec.io/blog/2026-03-03-zkvms-unfaithful-claims/

- **Author:** Defendor — DeFi Security
- **Media:** photo
- **Views:** 354

### Text

🛡️ Vulnerability in Anchor: Short Discriminators Can Disable Event Handling

A risk has appeared in Anchor: short custom discriminators without validation can shadow the system event handler, completely disabling it without any warnings.

This happens due to the lack of length checks and prefix overlaps, as the dispatcher first checks user custom discriminators before the system event tags.

Although attackers cannot change discriminators after compilation, this bug creates unpredictable behavior and "dead code" in programs.

Anchor plans to strengthen validations to avoid such accidental developer errors.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://exvul.com/blog/anchor-one-byte-discriminator-event-dispatch

The bug could have bypassed confidential transfer fee validation in Token-2022. The affected functionality was disabled following disclosure, and no exploitation was detected.

🔗 Details

### Links

- http://blog.zksecurity.xyz/posts/solana-phantom-challenge-bug/

### Text

🔴 Critical Bug in Aptos Move VM Allowed Arbitrary Storage Hijack

Hexens discovered a stale cache vulnerability in the Aptos Move VM where a partial cache flush wiped the struct name index map but left the type tag cache intact, letting an attacker recycle struct indices and redirect borrow_global_mut to a victim's storage slot.

In theory, the bug could have enabled vault draining, capability theft (SignerCapability, MintRef), and cross-chain damage via Wormhole and LayerZero bridges. A patch was deployed privately before public disclosure in February 2026.

🔗 Details

Attackers created a near-empty TUNA/USDC pool and routed 569,601 USDC borrowed from DefiTuna's lending pool through it, receiving so little TUNA back that the position's total value rounded down to zero in the solvency check, which then incorrectly treated the position as healthy.

With the borrow effectively uncollateralized, attackers withdrew the USDC through two malicious limit-order positions, splitting ~$383K and ~$184K across pivot wallets.

### Text

🔑 Critical Flaw in Zilliqa Ledger App Exposes Private Keys From On-Chain Data

A nonce-generation bug in the Zilliqa Ledger app has been present since 2019, causing Schnorr signatures to use predictably biased nonces with the top 64 bits fixed at zero, allowing private key recovery from just 5+ on-chain signatures using lattice reduction.

Affected keys cannot be secured by simply moving funds, as attackers can front-run transfers using the already-derived private key. Users should await official Zilliqa guidance before taking any action.

🔗 Details

DeBond's bond group registry allowed duplicate bond IDs, and its exchange logic counted exception matches rather than tracking unique membership, breaking the burn-before-mint assumption and letting an attacker mint unbacked bonds.

The attacker registered output groups with duplicate IDs, triggering skipped burns while minting new bonds, then sold them for 542,144 USDC from liquidity pools.

### Text

🔢 When a Bug Fix Introduced a Worse Bug: Double Rounding in Stake Pool Withdrawals

Adevar Labs found that a fix to a Solana stake pool withdrawal path introduced a double-rounding issue, where two sequential floor operations compounded in the same direction, causing each withdrawal to under-burn LP tokens and slowly dilute the exchange rate for all remaining depositors.

The fix was to eliminate the reverse conversion entirely, keeping the original lp_tokens_to_burn value untouched.

🔗 Details

### Text

🔐 Deep Dive: How Fogo Sessions Reduce Signing Friction Without Weakening Security

Fogo Sessions let users sign one structured intent granting temporary, scoped authority to a Session Account, enforced at the protocol level through instruction introspection that binds the session to exactly what the user signed.

Token operations, expiry checks, and program authorization are all validated at runtime through a modified SPL Token Program that natively recognizes session accounts.

### Text

🔓 Foxify USDC Vault Drained via Role ID Collision in Factory Contract

Foxify's Factory assigned the same role ID to both depositor permissions and privileged Storage contract access, letting an attacker spin up their own market, inherit the role, and write inflated balances directly to drain the vault with no collateral.

The fix splits the role into two with distinct bit layouts, ensuring user roles no longer authorize Storage writes.

🔗 Details

### Text

⚠️ Ravencoin Overflow Bug Causes Network Split Risk

A quantity overflow bug in Ravencoin's asset transfer logic allowed invalid blocks to pass conservation checks on unpatched nodes while being rejected by fixed ones, creating a chain split rather than an infinite mint.

The first known invalid block is 4,487,776, meaning everything after block 4,487,775 carries reorg risk until the network converges.

🔗 Details

A value can be computed correctly, pass all honest tests, and still provide no security if the system never constrained it to satisfy the intended invariant.

### Text

🥪 Arrakis Finance Hack Wasn't Oracle Manipulation

The exploit had nothing to do with spot price tricks — the vault's proportional mint/redeem design already blocks that attack vector.

The real flaw was in burn(): unclaimed LP fees get compounded back into the pool, letting the attacker sandwich a mint/burn sequence to pull out more tokens than deposited.

### Text

🔬 Hexens Breaks Down Why QKD's Proofs Are Solid, But Hardware Isn't

Hexens explains that QKD promises security from physics, but real attacks exploit gaps between the ideal protocol and actual equipment, like detector blinding attacks that steal keys undetected.

Fixes like decoy states and MDI-QKD close these gaps, though large real-world networks still rely on trusted relay nodes rather than pure physics-based security.

🔗 Details

### Links

- https://hexens.io/blog/quantum-key-distribution

### Text

🔓 Injective Exploit Traced to Market ID Collision Bug

Hashing market fields without separators let an INJ insurance fund collide with a USDC-denominated binary options market sharing the same market_id.

Self-trades manufactured a refund deficit, then the INJ fund covered a USDC shortfall by raw integer amount, skipping the haircut and letting the attacker withdraw inflated balances.

🔗 Details

### Text

🔓 Secured Finance Loses ~$104K in Self-Trade Price Manipulation

Collateral was valued off a same-block price formula that both sides of a trade can control, letting attackers self-trade the price up and withdraw the pool.

The original attacker's exploit reverted out of gas, but a frontrunning bot grabbed the largest slice (~0.9 WBTC) minutes later, forwarding most of it to a builder.

🔗 Details

🌊 Liquid Network Consensus Split Leads to ~4,000 BTC Peg-Out

A rangeproof-verification cache bug in some Elements Core builds may have let nodes accept a modified transaction context without full verification, causing a chain divergence.

The funds appear controlled by self-described "whitehats" who say they'll return them once all nodes are patched; Liquid has paused its bridge nodes.

### Text

🔓 Vulnerable BNB Router Loses ~62.28 WBNB to Fake Pool Callback

The router's callback pulled tokens via a caller-supplied payer without verifying the caller was a genuine pool, and its factory address was set to zero.

A single flash-swapped transaction deployed a fake pool and drained standing approvals from 29 users; anyone who ever approved this router was exposed.

🔗 Details
